Stacy Keach, ‘Mike Hammer’ and ‘Fat City’ Star, Dead at 85

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Stacy Keach, the actor whose turns as Hamlet on stage and hard-bitten detective Mike Hammer on television captivated audiences, died on Tuesday in Magdalenka, Poland, at 85.

His death was confirmed by his wife, Malgosia Tomassi Keach, who said he died at his family home. “After a long and difficult battle with a serious heart condition and heart failure, Stacy peacefully departed this world,” she said in a statement to The Hollywood Reporter. “He fought for so long, with extraordinary courage, determination and strength. He was a true warrior, facing his illness with the same remarkable spirit and resilience that defined his life.”

She continued, “Stacy was not only a legendary actor, admired and loved by audiences the world over, but also my beloved husband, my companion and the heart of our family. His passing leaves an immeasurable emptiness in our lives. But his extraordinary spirit, his unforgettable voice and the love he gave so generously will remain with us forever.”

Walter Stacy Keach Jr. was born on June 2, 1941, in Savannah, Georgia. His father was veteran character actor Stacy Keach Sr. and his mother was Mary Cain Peckham, the daughter of Texas oilman William H. Peckham. Along with his wife and their son, Shannon, and daughter, Karolina, he is also survived by his brother, actor James Keach. The siblings portrayed outlaw James brothers and also served as their own co-producers on the 1980 western The Long Riders.

Keach received a Tony nomination for portraying Buffalo Bill in the 1969 Broadway production Indians and went on to gain further acclaim as the fedora-wearing Hammer on the CBS series that ran from 1984 to 1987. He reprised the role in the 1989 TV movie Mike Hammer: Murder Takes All and another show, Mike Hammer, Private Eye, from 1997 to 1998.

Throughout his prolific career, Keach co-starred in a wide range of projects from movies The New Centurions, the John Huston-directed Fat City, and two Cheech and Chong films, Up in Smoke (1978) and Nice Dreams (1981). His depiction of Ernest Hemingway in the 1988 made-for-TV movie Hemingway earned him a Golden Globe Award.

He also won many stage accolades including Helen Hayes Awards in 1991 and 1996 for his work in Richard III and Macbeth with the Shakespeare Theatre Company. In 2015, he was inducted into the American Theatre Hall of Fame and honored with a star on the Hollywood Walk of Fame in 2019. Trending Stories Sofia Franklyn Reveals the Dark Side of ‘Call Her Daddy’ in Explosive Memoir: 9 Biggest Bombshells Judge Sets Steven Tyler Sex Assault Trial Date Paul McCartney and Ringo Starr to Reunite at Las Vegas Show in 2027 Watch Ariana Grande, Ben Stiller, Robert De Niro Take Over ‘The Tonight Show’

While Keach never reached superstardom, his career reflected his profound passion for both his craft and life. “If a script interests me, or a character intrigues me, or the cast features someone I’ve always wanted to work with, or the location is somewhere exotic, then sign me up,” Keach penned in his memoir All in All: An Actor’s Life On and Off the Stage. “I’m not going to wait for a call that may or may not come.”

8 Movies To Watch if You Love Ken Russell's 'The Devils'

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

In 17th-century France, Loudun became the site of the Loudun possessions, a gruesome case of alleged mass demonic possession that is now understood as a landmark study in mass hysteria. The "possessions" were blamed on Urbain Grandier, a charismatic but womanizing local priest who was then executed for political purposes. This event became the inspiration for Aldous Huxley's 1952 non-fiction book The Devils of Loudun, and then John Whiting's 1960 stage play The Devils. In 1971, Ken Russell took both of these works as inspiration and made The Devils.

One of the most notoriously heavily-censored horror movies in film history, The Devils was massively controversial upon release for its graphic sexual imagery and blasphemous content, but has since come to be recognized as one of the greatest films of its time. With the recent release of the 4K restoration of the film's 114-minute director's cut, now's about as perfect a time as any to watch the eight most noteworthy films that feel remarkably similar to The Devils for one reason or another.

As far as deliberately and calculatedly blasphemous films go, it doesn't get much more iconic than Viridiana, made by one of the most notoriously anti-clerical auteurs in film history, Luis Buñuel. Arguably the greatest of Buñuel's Mexican movies, Viridiana is one of the greatest movie classics of 1961, following a devout novice nun who abandons her vows to shelter local vagrants after the suicide of her fetishistic uncle.

Its surrealistic imagery is about as brilliant as you'd expect from any Buñuel film.

A must-see for all those whose favorite part of The Devils is the scathing study of The Profane as a philosophical concept, Viridiana is an unforgiving critique of religious dogma and naive idealism. Its surrealistic imagery is about as brilliant as you'd expect from any Buñuel film, and Silvia Pinal's lead performance is a tour de force. It's definitely more overtly sacrilegious than The Devils, but it's every bit as much of a hyper-influential must-see.

Before The Wizard of Oz came around to revolutionize fantasy cinema forever, there were many fantasy films that are also still remembered among the greatest of all time, including the horror essay film Häxan. This silent Swedish masterpiece is a semi-documentary blends scholarly literature and phantasmagoric horror, charting the history of witchcraft and Satanism from the Middle Ages to the 20th century.

It's one of the clearest and strongest cinematic ancestors to Ken Russell's magnum opus, and one of the most important documentaries in the history of cinema. Insightful without ever feeling didactic and atmospheric without ever sacrificing its scholarly dimensions, Häxan laid the foundations for horror cinema to explore themes of religious hysteria, state control through religion, and institutional persecution. Both through its visuals and its mere premise, it was far ahead of its time.

Robin Hardy's The Wicker Man is one of the greatest and most influential British horror movies of the 20th century, as well as one of the most important folk horror films of all time. It's based on David Pinner's horror novel Ritual, and it follows a devout Christian cop who travels to a remote Scottish island to investigate a young girl's disappearance, only to discover that the local pagan community has lured him there as a human sacrifice for a failing harvest.

Much like The Devils, The Wicker Man explores how sexual repression can be weaponized and exploited by oppressive forces; and much like The Devils, it forces the sacred and the profane to clash visually, narratively, and thematically throughout. It's one of those movies unlike anything you've ever seen before, a genre-defining masterpiece that shows how dogmatic self-righteousness can be little more than a self-destructive trap.

Four franchises, four completely different ways of getting underneath your skin. This is the one built out of your particular fear, why it works so well on you, and what it would leave behind.

Your nightmare does not wait outside the door. It waits for you to close your eyes, which you are going to have to do eventually, because nobody has ever won that particular argument.

Your nightmare is not really the doll. It is the face of the person you tell, and the way they decide, very kindly, that you have imagined all of it.

Your nightmare has your number, knows your name, and has almost certainly been inside your house before. The worst part is that you already know them.

Your nightmare is not really about you. It is about the people asleep down the hall, and the feeling that whatever is here is not interested in leaving.

What better recommendation for people who loved Ken Russell's The Devils than the rest of Russell's impeccable filmography? As it happens, after making one of the best horror movies of the 1970s, he made one of the best hard sci-fi movies of the 1980s: Altered States, based on Paddy Chayefsky's 1978 novel. In it, a Harvard psychophysiologist uses sensory deprivation tanks and powerful tribal hallucinogens to explore alternate states of consciousness, unwittingly triggering a dangerous devolution into a primitive ape-man.

Brilliantly experimental and surrealistic from beginning to end, Altered States is anchored by unparalleled visuals and sound design, creating a true aesthetic supernova further bolstered by John Corigliano's avant-garde score. Like all auteurs worth their salt, Russell had a collection of themes that were most interesting to him over the course of his career, and Altered States thus explores many ideas similar to those found in The Devils. Instead of 17th-century religion and politics, this film explores 20th-century scientific hubris, exploring ideas of repressed biological impulses through intentionally grotesque images.

Chilean-French avant-garde auteur Alejandro Jodorowsky is one of the biggest and most influential names in the history of surrealist cinema, and there are many who would point to The Holy Mountain as his magnum opus. After the underground success of the acid Western El Topo, Beatles manager Allen Klein agreed to produce Holy Mountain, and John Lennon and Yoko Ono also contributed financially. The result is one of the most delectably strange and fascinatingly impenetrable Mexican movies of all time, about seven people led up a sacred mountain by a powerful alchemist so they can steal the secret of immortality.

The Holy Mountain is certainly not for those who prefer their fantasy movies straightforward, and it's probably not the most accessible introduction to cinematic surrealism for those unfamiliar; but it's a must-see masterpiece nonetheless, especially for those who love the provocative blasphemy and hyper-stylized colors of The Devils. One of the best international masterpieces of all time, The Holy Mountain is just as fiercely anti-authoritarian as Russell's masterpiece, rejecting institutional corruption and repressive state control through a bunch of deliberately shocking moments.

There is perhaps no better Ken Russell film for people to watch after seeing (and loving) The Devils than Women in Love, which is also anchored by a powerhouse performance by Oliver Reed. This is not a horror movie, though it is a period piece, this one a romantic drama following the relationships between two sisters and two men in a mining town in post-World War I England. Based on the 1920 novel of the same title by D. H. Lawrence, the film obtained an impressive four Academy Award nominations, including Glenda Jackson's victory in the Best Actress category.

This was the movie that established Russell as a major force in world cinema, and it was its huge commercial and critical success that gave Warner the confidence to give Russell a blank check to make The Devils. These two films couldn't possibly be more different in terms of tone, with Women in Love being an uncompromisingly dense and philosophically profound adaptation of the source material. However, since Women in Love directly preceded The Devils, you can see many of the blueprints for Russell's next film there: eroticism, theatricality, and characters trapped between society's expectations and their own physical and psychological impulses.

Directed by Pier Paolo Pasolini and loosely adapted from the 1785 novel The 120 Days of Sodom by the Marquis de Sade, Salò, or the 120 Days of Sodom is perhaps one of the most controversial major films in the history of cinema. Taking the basis of the Marquis de Sade's story from 18th-century France to World War II Italy, it's about four wealthy fascists who kidnap a group of teenagers and subject them to 120 days of systemic mental, physical, and sexual torture inside a secluded villa. For its unapologetic approach to topics that continue to be taboo today, the film has been widely banned and censored across the world throughout history.

It isn't the least bit hard to see why. Much like The Devils, this is one of the heaviest movies of the 20th century, an absolutely unrelenting political allegory of fascism's cold cruelty shot with high-art anti-sensationalism that nonetheless doesn't make the film any easier to stomach. It's a remarkable feat that Pasolini was able to take such an inflammatory premise and manage to treat it with such care that it doesn't feel exploitative at all, a compliment that can also be directed at The Devils.

There are only two major feature films directly depicting the Loudun possessions, and the first one came out a decade before The Devils. It is, of course, the Polish horror art film Mother Joan of the Angels, directed by Jerzy Kawalerowicz and based on a novella of the same title by Jarosław Iwaszkiewicz. Winner of the Special Jury Prize at the 1961 Cannes Film Festival, Mother Joan of the Angels is one of those near-perfect horror movies that nobody remembers anymore, but which should be essential viewing for anyone even slightly interested in the story and context of the Loudun possessions.

Most would agree that the film isn't quite as masterful, timeless, or hard-hitting as The Devils, but it's still very much worthy of praise. With some incredibly striking black-and-white cinematography that stands in stark contrast to the vibrancy of Russell's masterpiece, Lucyna Winnicka's transcendental performance as Mother Joan, and a tremendous level of intellectual and existential depth, Mother Joan of the Angels is to the '60s what The Devils is to the '70s. Both are among the greatest "demonic possession" movies in the history of horror cinema.

In Kirk Cousins, the Vikings landed their first choice

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

After last night’s abysmal performance by the offense against the Bills, the Vikings mobilized to trade for a quarterback. Ultimately, they got the guy they wanted.

Per a source with knowledge of the situation, Kirk Cousins was Minnesota’s first choice.

The Vikings explored other options. Multiple players, including fellow former Vikings quarterback Daniel Jones.

Cousins’s history with the team makes it easier to get him up to speed. He had 25 regular-season games with coach Kevin O’Connell, and four seasons with receiver Justin Jefferson.

The existence of options may have helped the Vikings keep the compensation to only a third-round pick. Given the level of desperation, the Vikings seemed destined to give up more than that to fix a problem that threatened to implode the season.

That’s not an overstatement. The dam was breaking. Frustration was rampant during the debacle against the Bills, on both sides of the ball.

It was a simple proposition. If the Vikings didn’t trade for a quarterback on Tuesday, they could not be considered a serious franchise.

In the end, they managed to stop the bleeding by sending their third-round pick (their own, not the one they acquired from the Eagles for defensive end Jonathan Greenard) to the Raiders for Cousins.

It’s unclear how much better things will be with Cousins back in the fold. It can’t get worse than what they put on the field for Week 9.

VisionQuest Has A Post-Credits Scene (And It’s More Important Than You Think)

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Warning! This article contains SPOILERS for VisionQuest episode 8.The VisionQuest finale is now available on Disney+, and the episode adds to the long list of Marvel Cinematic Universe projects to feature a post-credits scene, which teases the future of a beloved character and breaks a negative trend. The MCU has made post-credit scenes a fan-favorite practice of superhero media, so whenever a genre movie or show does not have one, it is sorely missed.

There have been many memorable post-credits scenes throughout the MCU movie timeline, dating all the way back to Samuel L. Jackson's Nick Fury coming to Robert Downry Jr.'s Tony Stark to recruit him for the Avenger Initiative in 2008's Iron Man. More recently, many of the MCU TV shows on Disney+ in the Multiverse Saga have adhered to Marvel's post-credits scene practice.

Some of those have even directly set up new MCU movies, something that WandaVision, the first TV show in the trilogy that also features Agatha All Along and now comes to a close with Paul Bettany's VisionQuest, did for the controversial Doctor Strange in the Multiverse of Madness. Vision's MCU solo series follows suit after an exciting 8-episode run.

VisionQuest has felt like a breath of fresh air for the MCU, bringing back many AIs as human avatars and explaining where those come from, all while following White Vision's quest to feel human and connect the memories from the original Vision with real emotions. VisionQuest's finale ends with an emotional encounter, and its post-credits scene sets up another possible reunion.

Before going into the reunion set up by VisionQuest's post-credits scene, it is important to note that by including a sequence after the credits in the series finale, the latest MCU TV show on Disney+ has finally ended a disappointing 2026 Marvel streak. As mentioned before, post-credits scenes have become forever tied to the MCU experience, so whenever a movie or series does not feature them, it can be a little frustrating. Well, before VisionQuest, Marvel Studios released two live-action shows, Wonder Man, which had its season 2 renewal reversed, and Daredevil: Born Again season 2, with season 3 ending the series.

Choose the movie with the higher Rotten Tomatoes score.

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

Both Wonder Man and Daredevil: Born Again season 2 did not feature post-credits scenes, which is surprising, especially for the latter, given how season 1 teased where Jon Bernthal's Punisher would go next. That project was the live-action MCU Special Presentation The Punisher: One Last Kill, which also did not include a post-credits scene, even though Bernthal was set to return in Spider-Man: Brand New Day, so his MCU movie role could have been teased through an ending tag. Based on that, VisionQuest's post-credits scene breaks this negative MCU trend, being the only live-action Marvel release on the small screen in 2026 with a post-credits scene.

As for what happens in VisionQuest's post-credits scene, the sequence is focused on setting up the MCU future of James Spader's Ultron. After returning to the franchise for the first time since 2015's Avengers: Age of Ultron, the character was shown to have changed over the years he spent inside Vision's mind, leaving behind his desire to end humanity, even though the loss of Jocasta in the VisionQuest finale momentarily made him become a villain again before Vision forced Ultron to change course and reassess.

That is precisely what VisionQuest's post-credits scene is all about. Rather than featuring the return of Elizabeth Olsen's Scarlet Witch or setting up Vision's possible role in Avengers: Doomsday, the MCU TV show chose to tease another possible reunion and love story. Ultron is now pretending to be his human avatar, which VisionQuest revealed to be Virgil Horton, the grandson of scientist Phineas Horton.

Spader's Marvel character also carries with him Jocasta's hard drive, teasing that he will try to rebuild her as he spends some time getting to know humanity and one day get his happy ending, a poignant final note for VisionQuest, a show about finding your humanity. As to which project Ultron might return in, that is a mystery, though his more intimate story setup suggests another potential MCU series before a possible movie return, though the revival of a project like Armor Wars or having Ultron in an Iron Man movie after Avengers: Secret Wars soft reboots the MCU would make sense.

Ridley Scott’s 10/10 Sci-Fi Masterpiece Officially Won’t Return in 2026

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

2026 will be remembered as a bumpy year for the great Ridley Scott, who finally returned to sci-fi after nearly 10 years away from the genre. Scott tapped widely known stars, including Josh Brolin, Jacob Elordi, and even Margaret Qualley, to feature in The Dog Stars, but the film eked out only $39 million at the box office. The budget for the film was never publicly disclosed, but it was reported to be anywhere between $75 million and $100 million, which makes it a total box office disaster. Scott isn’t taking some time to reevaluate after the woeful performance of The Dog Stars, either – he’s returning to the screen next November for a Treasure Island reboot starring Hugh Jackman as the titular pirate. The movie is expected to begin shooting early next year, which will leave a tight window to hit its release date.

In this later stage of his career, Ridley Scott isn’t directing as many movies as he once was, but he remains involved in projects across the board through his Scott Free production banner. For example, it’s been almost 10 years since Scott directed his last Alien project, Alien: Covenant, but he produced the last few franchise installments, such as Alien: Romulus and Alien: Earth. The latter project, written and created by Fargo scribe Noah Hawley, premiered back in 2024 and was a massive hit with critics and audiences, prompting FX to bring the show back for Season 2. Production on Alien: Earth Season 2 kicked off earlier this year, and while there was an initial wave of optimism that the show would return before the end of 2026, it can now be safely assumed that it won’t be released until 2027.

Most of the stars whose characters survived the events of Season 1 are expected back in Season 2, but there are plenty of exciting new additions to the cast as well. Both Peter Dinklage and Jerome Flynn are among the new stars joining the Alien: Earth Season 2 cast, but this won’t be their first time working together. The duo spent years playing the pair of Tyrion Lannister and Bronn in Game of Thrones, the hit HBO fantasy series that was on the air from 2010 to 2019.

Check out the first season of Alien: Earth on Hulu or Disney Plus and stay tuned to Collider for more updates and coverage of the second season.

6 Forgotten Movie Trilogies Where Every Movie Is Near-Perfect

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Feel free to connect with him or check out his work. He's everywhere — Upwork, YouTube, Spotify, SoundCloud, Collider, LinkedIn, Instagram.

The best thing about a great trilogy is that it gets three chances to break a heart and somehow never wastes one. No middle chapter that feels like a detour, no final entry that leaves a viewer wishing the story had stopped earlier. Just three films that keep deepening the world they have made, until even a coal-strewn path or an empty dance floor starts to feel like a real place.

The six below are trilogies to hand over without a word. Some are funny in the way people get when they are afraid to be sincere, while others leave the room completely silent after the credits. Ranging from intimate black-and-white memories to a nine-hour march through war, each entry brings its own distinct weather and rhythm. They earn the rarest praise of all. Finish the last film, and the urge to restart is immediate. Allow me to give you near-perfect trilogies.

The people in Whit Stillman’s Metropolitan, Barcelona, and The Last Days of Disco don’t storm out of a party so much as spend the ride home explaining why they were right to stay. They are young, polished, and faintly terrified that history has made them ridiculous. Tom Townsend (Edward Clements) wanders into Manhattan debutante culture, Barcelona sends talkative young Americans abroad, The Last Days of Disco follows Alice (Chloë Sevigny) and Charlotte (Kate Beckinsale) beneath a glittering ceiling, trying to look at ease while the music changes around them.

The pleasure is in the small humiliations, a tuxedo being adjusted, a dance floor watched from its edge, an insult delivered with perfect manners. Beneath the talk, the comedy has real tenderness for those who think too much because they feel too much. Class anxiety and romantic failure keep poking holes in their careful arguments. Unapologetically literary and tenderly funny, the trilogy feels like a time capsule from before everyone learned to perform their lives for a screen.

Jamie (Stephen Archibald) scavenges for coal in a Scottish mining village, and the sound of his worn boots crossing the grit feels almost too loud for the silence around him. His half-brother Tommy (Hughie Restorick) also features in the trilogy, My Childhood, My Ain Folk, and My Way Home, which follows Jamie through poverty, bereavement, neglect, and the uneasy possibility of escape. Jamie rarely explains what he feels. You see it in his watchful eyes, his hunched shoulders, the way he occupies a cold room as if apologizing for taking up space.

Yet that quietness is precisely what ruins you. In stark black and white, a face can hold the whole weight of a family that has failed it. The films just lay the hardship out raw and let the silence sit until it hurts. Then, in the final chapter, the world opens a fraction through military service in Egypt and the pull of art. Unsparingly intimate, these three films feel less like a life retold than a piece of one carefully preserved.

A boy wants to return his friend’s notebook. That is the small, urgent engine of Where Is the Friend’s House? It follows Ahmed (Babak Ahmadpour), who climbs and doubles back along the zigzag paths above Koker, asking after a door, a name, a child. The walk is so simple that you begin to feel every slope under his feet. Then Life, and Nothing More... returns to the region after the 1990 earthquake, and Through the Olive Trees follows a film crew and young Hossein (Hossein Rezai), who continues pursuing Tahereh (Tahereh Ladanian) despite her reluctance to return his affection. Fiction starts to brush against real lives without claiming to explain them.

That shifting boundary is the trilogy’s quiet astonishment. Damaged roads and ordinary conversations are treated with patience, never turning disaster into a backdrop for easy uplift. Dust clings. Everyone keeps moving, searching, rebuilding, and questioning. Somehow, that winding hillside holds everything these films have to say about distance, pursuit, and hope. Profoundly humane, these films make persistence feel like an everyday act of courage.

In the Trilogy of Silence, Voyage to Cythera, The Beekeeper, and Landscape in the Mist all follow wanderers who keep moving because stopping would mean admitting how far they have been left behind. An exiled old communist named Spyros (Manos Katrakis) returns to Greece, another man named Spyros (Marcello Mastroianni) travels through the country as a beekeeper, and two children, Voula (Tania Palaiologou) and Alexandros (Michalis Zeke), set off toward Germany in search of a father who may be more hope than destination. The plots matter, but the landscapes stay with you, wet roads, empty ports, gray stations, a small figure crossing a field of fog.

Theo Angelopoulos gives those spaces time to breathe. His long takes make you feel the distance between strangers, and the slow walk through rain becomes its own kind of ache. These are not films that rush to console you. History hangs over every encounter, and the weather seems to know it. Yet the journeys keep drawing the eye forward. Mystically patient, the trilogy lodges in memory through its yearning for connection. A hand held out in the mist, a yellow coat against the gray, someone still waiting to be found. It hits and it stays.

The “economic miracle” looks immaculate. Not here. Rainer Werner Fassbinder gets close enough to see the powder, the cigarette ash, and the exhausted smile. In The Marriage of Maria Braun, Lola, and Veronika Voss, three women navigate West Germany’s postwar boom, each finding a different price attached to survival. Maria (Hanna Schygulla) builds herself into a force. Lola (Barbara Sukowa) turns desire into a transaction. Veronika (Rosel Zech), once a star, drifts toward ruin. Their worlds gleam with cabaret light and polished surfaces, but every room feels like a deal being negotiated.

These women are fiercely alive, driven by sharp appetites and quiet desperation. You catch it in the forced smile, the twitch of a cigarette, the restless look toward a clock while a man talks. Romance and loyalty are never free of money or power. Ruthlessly funny, then suddenly heartbreaking, the trilogy uses melodrama’s sumptuous colors to expose a country trying to buy its way past its own history. In fact, all that shine only sharpens the damage. It sticks with you.

Kaji (Tatsuya Nakadai) believes humane treatment can survive inside a labor camp. That belief is the first thing the machinery of war tries to kill. Across The Human Condition trilogy, an idealistic pacifist is dragged from Manchurian mines into the Imperial Japanese Army, then toward Soviet captivity and a desperate march through winter. The scale is enormous, but the hurt stays close, a hollow face, frozen limbs dragging through snow, Michiko’s (Michiyo Aratama) love held in memory like a hand he might still reach.

This trilogy refuses the comfort of heroic war stories. Orders grind people down, the men who obey them are themselves afraid, hungry, and trapped. Black dirt and blood stain the white landscape, and every bootstep seems to cost Kaji something he cannot get back. The nine-hour journey asks you to sit with that cost. It demands it. Kaji carries the films through a transformation that grows more worn and more stubborn by the hour. Uncompromisingly humane, this trilogy turns endurance into a moral question, how much of yourself can you lose and still keep walking? You keep walking.

Steven Spielberg’s 10-Part Sci-Fi Thriller Is Perfect for ‘Person of Interest’ Fans

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

15 years ago, CBS delivered one of its best procedurals with Person of Interest. The series, which focuses on a mysterious "Machine" and a former Special Forces operative tasked by the Machine's creator to protect the people it identifies, perfectly blends a high concept sci-fi pitch with the standard procedural formula. Like Lost before it, Person of Interest saw a wake of similar shows premiering after its debut, most notably Almost Human. One series boasts the pedigree of being a direct sequel to one of the best sci-fi movies of the 21st century.

That series is Minority Report, which picks up in the aftermath of Steven Spielberg's sci-fi thriller. The Minority Report series follows Dash (Stark Sands), a former precognitive whose visions were used by the "Precrime" police department to stop crimes before they happen. Haunted by visions of murders yet to happen, Dash contacts Detective Lara Vega (Meagan Good) to help stop those crimes before they happen. Though it only lasted a single season, Minority Report had similar vibes to Person of Interest, including deep questions about the nature of justice.

Part of the draw of Minority Report is the dynamic between Dash and Vega, especially as she gets used to the fact that he can see the future and transitions into a normal life while still using his abilities to help others. The episode "The Present" is perhaps the strongest example of this dynamic at work, as Dash gets a flash of Vega's death and works to solve it while she deals with the unresolved matter of her father's death. Another element that makes Minority Report stand out is its approach to the future, especially since the series takes place 11 years after the movie. Spielberg was ahead of the curve by asking scientists what technology would look like in the 2050's, and showrunner Max Borenstein said he took a similar approach when envisioning his take on Minority Report.

"One of the refreshing things about the film is that it didn't posit a single catastrophic event that led to the future. It said, "50 years from now, what is the world going to look like?" And the answer was that the world is going to be good and bad in equal measure to today. We have horrible thing that happen in our world, so I don't think we need to conjure up an apocalypse on our show to have real stakes."

Minority Report would also continue to incorporate elements from the movie, including Dash's siblings Arthur (Nick Zano) and Agatha (Laura Reagan). Both of them have wildly different visions; while Arthur gains knowledge of people who are about to be murdered, Agatha ends up psychically stepping in their shoes, which is just as horrifying as it sounds. The duo ends up helping Dash and Vega, but they have to be careful, as mysterious forces are hunting all three precogs to use their abilities. If Person of Interest is a story about the dangers of unchecked technology, then Minority Report asks what the law will do to keep people safe.

Despite being based on one of Steven Spielberg's most iconic movies, and even boasting Spielberg as an executive producer, Minority Report only lasted a single season. The deck was stacked against it from the start, as Fox cut the first season's episode order from thirteen episodes to ten and had it airing in the same time slot as NBC's The Voice, which was at the height of its popularity. Bad reviews didn't help, either, as many critics felt that Minority Report lacked the spark of the movie that inspired it.

The cast and crew of Minority Report has managed to find success with other sci-fi projects. Max Borenstein helped kick off the MonsterVerse movies, Nick Zano would become an integral part of Legends of Tomorrow, and Li Jun Li would go on to headline buzzy projects like Sinners and Spider-Noir. Still, for a series that was about predicting the future, Minority Report never saw its own end coming.

‘Tangles’ Cast Members Seth Rogen, Julia Louis-Dreyfus & Bryan Cranston On Animated Film’s Rare Back Story

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

An animated film takes time to make. An animated film based on a funny-but-deeply-moving graphic memoir about a family’s experience with early-onset Alzheimer’s disease can take a bit longer.

Lauren Miller Rogen, wife of Seth Rogen and one of the producers of the film Tangles, told the crowd at Monday night’s special screening of the film in New York that work began on the adaptation back in 2016. It was acquired earlier this year by IFC, which will release it for a qualifying run later this month ahead of a wider bow in early 2027.

“I saw so much of my own story in [author] Sarah Levitt’s family story,” Rogen said. “I have a feeling a lot of you will see your own stories in it as well.” Related Stories News AMC Global Media Posts Mixed Q3, Previews 'The Walking Dead' AMC+ Bow News Martin Scorsese Explains How Seth Rogen "Ruined" His 'Cleopatra' Film: "Kim Jong Un Doesn't Have A Sense Of Humor"

The regrettable ubiquity of dementia and Alzheimer’s, along with the potency of Levitt’s work, drew a crowd of A-listers to the project. The voice cast includes Seth Rogen, Bryan Cranston, Julia Louis-Dreyfus, Abbi Jacobson and Beanie Feldstein. Producers include Louis-Dreyfus; Rogen via his company, Point Grey; Monarch Media, Giant Ant; and LYLAS Pictures. Watch on Deadline

During a Q&A after the screening, Louis-Dreyfus said the black-and-white, 2-D film “took advantage of the art form in an unusual and whatever-the-opposite-of-derivative-is way was very appealing to me. I think it’s very funny and very devastating. I related to it from a personal point of view, having Alzheimer’s in my own family, not once but twice. So, I felt like I understood it and the story needed to be told, and I was honored to be a part of it.”

Cranston observed, “It’s not always the case when you get contacted by people you know and friends saying, ‘We’d like you to be in our movie’ that it’s good.” As the audience laughed, Seth Rogen cut in: “Almost never!” Cranson, with half-joking affirmation from Louis-Dreyfus, continued, “You go, ‘Whoa, how do I get out of this?!’ And then you say something like, ‘You know, I didn’t connect with the character as much as, someone else is going to really, uh …’ But I didn’t have to do any of that. I read the script and I thought, ‘Wow, this is beautiful.'”

Jacobson voices the lead role of Sarah, who at the time the story starts in 1999 is a young artist living in San Francisco, 3,000 miles from her family, exploring a new relationship with a character voiced by Samira Wiley. “I’m playing a real person, who I got to meet. And I read her book, and so that carried a lot of weight for me,” Jacobson Said. A “rarity,” she added, were voice recording sessions that involved multiple cast members performing together. “To get to be in a room doing the voiceover with them is such a different experience. You get to look the other actor in the eye and you get to act and be present. And with a story that’s really important. There’s a lot going on, and it felt so connected, same as the comedic moments. It’s always better when you’re in the room.”

Monday’s event at Regal Union Square was presented by Hilarity for Charity, a not-for-profit founded by the Rogens to support families affected by Alzheimer’s. Joining cast members were Leah Nelson, the film’s director, producer and co-writer; and Levitt, who co-wrote the screenplay. Guests also included former AMC Global Media CEO Josh Sapan, whose company, Sapan Studios, teamed with IFC on the acquisition. (Sapan knows plenty about lengthy film productions, having fostered the Oscar-winning Boyhood during his AMC tenure.) Current AMC Global execs were also out in force, among them CEO Kristin Dolan, Chief Content Officer Dan McDermott; and IFC Entertainment Group chief Scott Shooman.

The 10 Most Intense Movie Scenes of All Time, Ranked

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Looking over some of the most intense movie scenes of all time is also going to reveal a bunch of movies that themselves can be counted as the most intense of all time. Overlap’s inevitable, but the focus below will be on those individual scenes. Sure, a scene can be long, taking up 10 to 20 percent of a movie’s runtime in some cases, or if it’s suspenseful for a shorter amount of time, maybe it’s an especially harrowing two to three minutes.

Also, talking about specific scenes will mean spoilers can be found below, but not every scene here necessarily takes place near a film’s conclusion. And for one more “also,” these movies have all been out for at least a few years, and most are quite well-known, so you're unlikely to have too much genuinely spoiled here, if you're interested enough in movies overall to read (or skim) a ranking like this.

Since 2027 is less than two months away, at the time of writing, Children of Men might well be an even more stomach-churning watch nowadays than it might've been back in 2006. Most dystopian movies are intense in one way or another, for hopefully obvious reasons, but the bleak future (which isn't a future for much longer) of Children of Men feels especially so.

The first scene is shocking, as are two set pieces that involve a car (one of them in the car, and one involving a car that doesn’t start), yet the final sequence, which sees the surviving characters (not too many) caught up in an all-out war zone, is the most full-on in Children of Men. It contains the most elaborate of the movie’s long takes, and it does an uncomfortably good job at making you feel like you're caught up in all the horror and bloodshed yourself.

There was a time when the shower scene in Psycho might've been genuinely surprising, but nowadays, you know, it’s the shower scene from Psycho. It comes before the halfway point of the movie, and it features the only character who’s been in the entire movie, scene by scene, up until that point. And out of nowhere, she’s murdered in a way that was very shocking by the standards of the early 1960s.

It’s a real shock to the system, or would've been before it became legendary, and so for all that, this scene’s well and truly earned its reputation.

It’s about as famous as thriller movie scenes get, and it played a part in rewriting the rules of what you could and couldn’t do early on in a movie. It’d be shocking enough to have the shower scene happen at the end, but when it happens where it does here, it’s like, “Wow, there’s an hour left, and now literally anything can happen… and actually, what even can happen?” It’s a real shock to the system, or would've been before it became legendary, and so for all that, this scene’s well and truly earned its reputation as an all-time great moment in cinema history.

Alfred Hitchcock got a shout-out here by highlighting Psycho, and then David Fincher, who’s also got a knack for directing suspenseful and effortlessly intense movies, is going to be mentioned shortly. In similar territory is Brian De Palma, and it feels necessary to highlight at least one scene he’s done as being especially intense, so of all the candidates (often finales; see Carrie and Scarface), here’s the big shootout in The Untouchables.

Well, it’s kind of big. The gunfight itself doesn't technically last too long, but The Untouchables spends an agonizingly long time building it all up, and then the use of slow motion during the build-up and when the shooting itself starts also draws the scene out in the best of ways. It’s such an expertly done set piece, and even if De Palma might have some movies that are darker and more consistently tense than The Untouchables, it’s this single scene that best demonstrates his knack for crafting cinematic suspense.

Make no mistake: pretty much the entirety of Zodiac is tense, and then when it’s not tense, it’s more disquieting and despairing. The search for the Zodiac Killer is at the core of the story, unsurprisingly, and most of the tense parts play out when the killer himself is at large, with the more subtly uneasy parts being found in the back half, when the chances of actually finding the killer seem to dissipate further scene by scene.

The terror does come back in full force, though, with the infamous basement scene in Zodiac, which happens closer to the end and involves Robert Graysmith possibly encountering the killer, but the not knowing – and the darkness of the sequence visually – bring the film the closest it gets to outright horror territory. It’s far from the only memorable scene in Zodiac, of course, but it does leave the biggest impact.

The scene you probably remember as being the opening one, even if Saving Private Ryan does start decades after the end of World War II before flashing back, it’s that famous extended sequence depicting the D-Day Landing at Omaha Beach. Guess it is chronologically the first scene that takes place in Saving Private Ryan, and it does have such an impact that it sticking in one’s mind over the actual opening scene is understandable.

There is also a large-scale battle sequence near the end of Saving Private Ryan, and that’s no slouch in terms of showing some truly visceral combat either. However, it's this opening sequence that proves most startling, coming earlier in the film, and it’s also a depiction of an actual battle, so it has that extra sense of dread and terror to it (the final battle is fictionalized, though it does aim to convey World War II-era combat nonetheless).

There are pictures here. You need pictures in an article, apparently. There was an effort made with all the other movies to source relevant pictures (you know, not just showing any moment from Saving Private Ryan or Psycho, but screenshots that convey the scene in question and the relevant intensity). With The Silence of the Lambs, though, the notably tense sequence – or at least the most intense part of that sequence – is memorably very dark, and it’s harder to make things out when it’s not in motion.

It involves Clarice Starling finally confronting Buffalo Bill, and eventually thwarting him, but not until after he switches off all the lights and stalks her around his already creepy house/basement while wearing night-vision goggles. It’s perhaps the part when The Silence of the Lambs most feels like a horror movie, though any big scene with Hannibal Lecter is, quite naturally, also frightening (an honorable mention should go out to his big escape sequence).

You do know this one is coming at all points, and sure, there is even some relief when it happens, just because 127 Hours so perfectly captures – and makes you feel – the utterly nightmarish situation its central character is in throughout. He is hiking without sufficient company and safety equipment, and ends up trapped when a boulder falls on his arm, crushing it and then ultimately not being movable.

So, after several days, he makes the decision to amputate his arm with the very few pieces of equipment he has nearby, and it is what saves his life, sure, but it does also prove nauseating to watch because nothing is shied away from. It’s one of the most painful-to-watch scenes in relatively recent memory, yet it has to be, and there is an understandably great sense of relief alongside the absolute nausea you'll feel witnessing it all.

Jaws is another Steven Spielberg movie that, like Saving Private Ryan, technically begins near or on a beach, and also in a manner that proves surprising and famously intense. That first shark attack scene is monumental, as are a bunch of the sequences that follow throughout the first hour or so of Jaws, though the final act is where the movie obviously crescendos, and if you want to call it the “final showdown,” then that’s it. That’s what’s going here.

You’ve got a whole movie of things not really letting up, and it’s still all quite patiently done, not to mention in a manner that makes being truly invested in that climax inevitable. Jaws is – and forever will be – the best movie about three guys who have to go out to sea and kill a shark, and this extended climax is a big reason why.

Everything in Inglourious Basterds is pretty great, and it’s also a film that has a handful of standout scenes that all manage to feel incredibly tense, but usually in different ways, so there’s no real repetition here. It’s longer than a fair few Quentin Tarantino movies, albeit paced in a way that really makes it all fly by, and all the big scenes help immensely in that regard.

That does also make highlighting one over all the others tough, but here, the one that’s going to be singled out is the tavern sequence. All the other show-stopping scenes are perfect and 10/10-worthy, with this one, like, being an 11/10 scene. Movies don’t get much more nerve-wracking than this, and it’s a shame you can only ever see something for the first time just the once, since being able to experience a scene this perfectly surprising again would be wonderful.

There’s no shark here, but Whiplash does a similar thing to Jaws in terms of having suspense and unease pretty much right from the start, and then having those feelings become more intense as things progress. You're not given a lot of time to breathe, and the dynamic between a dedicated student and a tyrannical instructor gets impossibly uncomfortable, not to mention psychologically harrowing, continually reaching new heights… or lows?

Anyway, that does result in the climax being the biggest and most jaw-dropping sequence in the movie. Whiplash also twists the knife after it’s over, since the movie leaves you hanging in terms of the implications of the eventual moment of triumph, or tragedy. Or it could be a bit of both. It’s hard to know. You'll never know. So you're on the edge of your seat for the final 10 to 15 minutes of Whiplash, and then you continually feel queasy for the next 10 to 15 years after Whiplash came out, whenever you think about it and its ending. That is undoubtedly impressive stuff for a movie’s ending to do.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Henry Cavill's The Witcher Replacement Has One Major Advantage Over Geralt Of Rivia

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

After starring on Netflix's The Witcher for three seasons, Henry Cavill's big role in an upcoming movie is the perfect next step in the actor's career, with one major advantage over his previous fantasy part. Before Liam Hemsworth took over the role of Geralt in season 4, Cavill had become synonymous with the character, making his exit so upsetting.

The world of The Witcher is bigger than any one cast member. The Witcher: Sirens of the Deep had a different Geralt, voiced by Doug Cockle, and built on a legacy that lasted even longer than Cavill had in the part. Cockle previously voiced Geralt of Rivia in all three The Witcher video games, helping to craft the character onscreen.

Considering that the games themselves informed much of the look and feel of the series, and were themselves drawn from The Witcher novels, that world is actually large enough to last without Henry Cavill. And the actor's talents have now been pointed toward a perfect role, with a character who has one major advantage over Geralt of Rivia.

Cavill's career in Hollywood is sure to be long and full of fantastic roles, but this upcoming project could become a defining film for the star.

After having played such iconic roles as Superman and Sherlock Holmes, his new take on another popular and beloved part could be the perfect chance for Cavill to do something truly unique, thanks to an incredible ability.

Henry Cavill will star in the upcoming remake of Highlander, where his lead character, Connor MacLeod, will have a power that no other characters in Cavill's past have. Connor MacLeod is an Immortal and serves as the center of the story as the titular Highlander, who is set to one day fight for The Prize.

While there is an inherent tension to Connor's existence, as captured by the original 1986 Highlander film's popular quote, "There can be only one", this is still an incredible power.

Born in the 16th century in Scotland, Connor has spent centuries exploring the Earth and training as a swordsman in order to one day be able to fight to become the last of his kind.

Connor has possessed some powers in addition to his immortality. During the Quickening, when an Immortal is killed, generally by another Immortal, a surge of energy escapes their body, allowing the victor to absorb the strength and memories of their fallen foe. This adds to a powerset that also includes enhanced regeneration.

Still, even with all of Connor's fantastic abilities, the character is important to compare against the roles that Cavill has played in the past. And while Geralt of Rivia might not be immortal, he does possess some fantastic skills that might make him even stronger than Connor could ever hope to be.

Geralt of Rivia actually shares some similarities in his powers with Connor MacLeod. While not hampered by a connection to a race of Immortals, which is itself an asset to Geralt's mortality, The Witcher's protagonist also has the benefit of slow aging, as well as tremendous skills in regeneration and healing.

Geralt, another trained swordsman, has greater speed, skill, and reflexes than Connor has generally been depicted as having. A fantastic tracker with elevated senses, Geralt has also been mutated in several ways that contribute to his incredibly enhanced physical powers of movement, fighting, and more.

If Geralt of Rivia and Connor MacLeod ever met in battle, it is hard to say exactly who would emerge triumphant, and much of that will depend on how the upcoming film depicts Connor. However, the more battle-hardened and practiced Geralt of Rivia would likely have at least a small advantage over his Highlander foe.

Regardless, both characters are excellent parts for Henry Cavill. And while The Witcher will continue to live on without him in some form, even after the Netflix series ends, this new and thrilling story of a rebooted Highlander is only about to get started.

Meghan's father Thomas Markle back in federal custody after allegedly removing ankle monitor

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Thomas Markle was placed under house detention at his assisted living facility in Florida last week

Thomas Markle, the father of Meghan, Duchess of Sussex, is back in federal custody in Florida after allegedly removing his ankle monitor, according to court documents.

Markle appeared before a federal magistrate judge in the Florida city of Tampa on Tuesday and was denied bond. Prosecutors say he removed the monitor early that morning.

US Judge Lindsay S Griffin found on Tuesday that Markle had violated the conditions of his pretrial release, court documents show.

Markle, who has denied possessing images of child sex abuse, was arrested in Florida last week and placed under house detention. He was also ordered to wear a location monitoring device.

The BBC has contacted his representatives.

Griffin described Markle's housing situation as "untenable", writing in the order revoking his pretrial release that "based on the lack of suitable housing, no condition or combination of conditions would reasonably assure the safety of any other person or the community and the defendant's appearance in court".

He also ordered a medical evaluation for Markle and said he "must receive all necessary and prescribed medication unless discontinued by a physician".

"Release conditions are court orders, not suggestions," Bill Essayli, the US attorney for Central California, wrote on X on Tuesday.

"We will continue to prosecute this case to the fullest extent of the law," he added.

Markle, 82, was first arrested last week after the FBI, acting on warrants, searched storage units in Los Angeles registered to him which he had rented since 2008, with the last payment being made in October this year, the DoJ said.

Inside the units the FBI says agents seized about 78 items, including film negatives, slides and books that they allege contained child sex abuse material.

Witnesses "positively identified" Markle in two of the images believed to contain child sex abuse material.

In those images, the man pictured "appears to be in the age range of 30s to 40s", which suggests the alleged abuse dates back decades as Markle is currently 82 years old.

Markle appeared in court last week following his arrest. No plea was taken during the appearance, which set several conditions for his pretrial release, including that he be kept in home detention.

He is charged with one count of possessing indecent images of children, according to the DoJ.

FBI director Kash Patel said last week that Markle "allegedly targeted innocent children and sought to steal their innocence through his criminal actions".

Markle has called the allegations "sick lies".

He moved to the Philippines last year after he had a series of strokes, and lived in Cebu until May. He was reportedly learning to walk again in Florida after having his leg amputated.

The duchess has been estranged from her father since 2018. The BBC understands she broke contact with him last year, telling him she and the authorities knew he had abused young girls.

In a previously shared statement, Meghan confirmed the existence of the letter and wrote: "I am deeply distressed... my thoughts are with any children who may have been harmed."

She said she hoped it could be understood why she had largely cut off contact from her father and did "not allow him to have contact with our children".

JoBe Cerny, Voice of the Pillsbury Doughboy, Dies at 78

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

JoBe Cerny, who voiced the giggling Pillsbury Doughboy character in recent years, died on Oct. 30. He was 78.

Born and raised in Cicero, Illinois, Cerny earned a bachelor’s degree in speech and drama from Valparaiso University and a master’s degree in theater from Northwestern University. He used the skills he learned to cultivate the voice behind ubiquitous advertisements such as Pillsbury Doughboy and the Silent Demonstrator for Cheer Detergent. Related Stories Amazon CEO Says Prime Video Is Profitable and Generates 'Tens of Billions of Dollars' Annually; Content Spending Grew 7% in Q3 to $5.9 Billion 'Carrie' Renewed for Season 2 at Amazon

In 2014, Cerny took over the Doughboy voice role from Paul Frees and later Jeff Bergman. The character is currently voiced by Rich Orlow.

His decades-long career included scores of roles in commercials, movies, television and theater. Of his career, Cerny once said, “I’m lucky, I get to be somebody different every day and in the end, I’m glad I stuck with my irresponsible decision to have fun. It allows me to live happily ever after … every day.”

Cerny also had small roles in films including “Road to Perdition,” “Legally Blonde 2,” “My Best Friend’s Wedding” and “Somewhere in Time.”

Cerny began acting when he joined The Second City as part of both the Mainstage and National Touring Company in the ’70s. He also owned and operated Cerny/American Creative Recording Studio in the River North neighborhood of Chicago for over 30 years, where he completed thousands of projects ranging from commercials and audiobooks to feature films.

Along with acting, Cerny was an avid racing fan who continued to drive go-karts into his 60s. He was also an enthusiastic model train collector. Those close to him remember his “friendly and gregarious personality, and extraordinary ability to coincidentally run into someone he knew almost everywhere he went.”

Cerny is survived by his wife of 56 years, Eileen Cerny; two sons, Tim (Andrea) Cerny and Patrick (Lily) Cerny; and grandchildren, Archer and Cleo.

Christina Applegate Thinks Breast Implants Are Making Her Sick

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

After Christina Applegate shared she was misdiagnosed with multiple sclerosis, she questioned if her breast implants could be the cause of her health issues.

Christina Applegate is still on a quest for answers.

Weeks after the Married…With Children alum shared that she had been misdiagnosed with multiple sclerosis, she began questioning if the breast implants she received after her 2008 breast cancer diagnosis could be causing her health issues.

“I’ve been thinking this for years,” Applegate shared with her MeSsy podcast host Jamie-Lynn Sigler on the Nov. 10 episode. “Is the breast implant poisoning? Because I actually had my double mastectomy in 2008 and I am way overdue for having them replaced.”

The 54-year-old added, “I swear, over the last five years or whatever, I thought, ‘What if it's seriously, what if they're leaking and they're poisoning me?’”

Her thoughts on the matter were also amplified after reading a study about breast implant poisoning “presenting as MS.”

“So that's one that I will be looking into,” Applegate noted, “because it's something I've thought about.”

The Dead to Me actress—who shares 15-year-old daughter Sadie with husband Martyn LeNoble—has been candid about her health struggles since she first shared her MS diagnosis in 2021. However, now that she’s learned she was misdiagnosed, she’s struggling to find out what is actually causing her issues.

"This may come as great news,” she said on the Oct. 20 episode of MeSsy, “but to me it comes as, like, I'm back at square one.”

That’s why when her doctor "came into my room and said, 'Congratulations, you don't have MS,’” Applegate recalled, "I was, like, not happy at all. Everybody else was so happy."

She also felt particularly discouraged as she explained to Sigler that she hasn’t “gotten better,” adding,  "If anything, I've gotten worse. So I'm back at square one where I don't know what's going on with me."

And she knew it would be hard for doctors to pinpoint a new diagnosis as she has “every symptom of someone with MS,” noting that there are many illnesses that can mirror MS.

Still, despite not having the disease, she shared that she was grateful to the MS community and continues to feel a part of it, despite her misdiagnosis.

“You guys are my people,” Applegate said on MeSsy Oct. 27. “Even though I don’t have this disease, I have fallen in love with everyone that we know—listeners and our friends—that have MS and I’m still your warrior.”

“I’m still going to speak out on your behalf for ever and ever,” she assured. “I still feel like part of this community. No matter what, I love you.”

For a look at Applegate’s health journey over the years, keep reading.

Christina Applegate shared she was diagnosed with multiple sclerosis in 2021.

"Hi friends. A few months ago I was diagnosed with MS," she shared on her social media channels that August. "It's been a strange journey. But I have been so supported by people that I know who also have this condition. It's been a tough road. But as we all know, the road keeps going. Unless some a--hole blocks it."

"Oh, by the way, I have a disease," she joked during her November 2022 Hollywood Walk of Fame induction ceremony, her first public appearance since her MS diagnosis. "Did you not notice? I'm not even wearing shoes."

Applegate believes her MS journey actually began "six or seven years" before her 2021 diagnosis.

"I noticed, especially the first season [of Dead to Me], we'd be shooting and my leg would buckle," Applegate explained during a March 2024 interview on Good Morning America. "I really just put it off as being tired, or I'm dehydrated, or it's the weather. Then nothing would happen for months, and I didn't pay attention."

By the time she was shooting the Netflix series' third and final season, the actress said she was "being brought to set in a wheelchair."

"I couldn't move that far," she recalled, "so I had to tell everybody because I needed help."

The Dead to Me star captioned this photo of her cane collection amid her battle with MS: "Walking sticks are now part of my new normal."

Joined by her daughter Sadie Grace LeNoble, Applegate had a simple message for multiple sclerosis while attending the 2023 SAG Awards: "F U MS."

And she's not the only one experiencing strong feelings towards the illness. "In my situation, Sadie only knew me as healthy and a runner and a Pelotoner and a dancer—and she only knew that," Applegate explained to friend Jamie-Lynn Sigler on an August 2025 episode of their MeSsy podcast. "So then when this came about in 2021, she was, like, stoic about it."

Yet, "I see her look at me when I’m in bed," Applegate continued, "and can't quite move or, I want to go say goodnight to her in her room but I can’t quite get down the hallway for whatever reason my legs aren’t working that day."

The whole experience has "broken" her teen, Applegate noted. "It was like losing the mom she had to this f--king thing. And the more she’s gotten older now, I think the more it’s hurting her."

After a receiving standing ovation at the 2023 Emmys, the Married...With Children alum quipped, "You're totally shaming me with disability by standing up."

"I make these jokes because if I don't, I'll suffocate," Applegate shared on a March 2024 episode on Armchair Expert, explaining why she often pokes fun at her condition. "I'll be done."

So, yes, she's going to talk about the fact that she's already purchased burial plots.

"My friend and I are going to go take a picnic there," Applegate revealed on a March 2026 episode of NPR's Wild Card. "It's really pretty where it is." She just has to pick out a tree "'cause it's really sunny," she continued. "And I want my visitors to not be sweating."

"I have 30 lesions on my brain," she noted on Armchair Expert. "My biggest one is behind my right eye, so my right eye hurts a lot."

Legions are caused by the immune system attacking the myelin sheath around nerves, according to the Multiple Sclerosis Trust.

Applegate hasn’t held back when it comes to sharing her journey.

"It sucks," the Anchorman actress told late night talk show host Jimmy Kimmel of her daily struggles with MS. "I'm not going to lie. I think anyone who has MS isn't going to be like, 'This is the best thing that ever happened to me!'"

Besides, she'd taken that tack when discussing her breast cancer battle back in 2008. "I went out, and I was the good girl talking about 'Oh, I love my new boobs' that are all scarred and f--ked up. What was I thinking?" she said on Armchair Expert. "Everything I was saying was a freaking lie. It was me trying to convince myself of something, and I think that did no service to anyone."

While she started a foundation and "raised millions of dollars for women to get MRIs who were at high risk," she noted, "at the back of it, I was taking off my bra and crying every night. And I wish that I had said that."

When talking about her day-to-day life with the disease, Applegate told Good Morning America in March 2024, "I live kind of in hell."

"But I might get to a place where I function a little bit better," she added. "Right now, I'm isolating, and that's kind of how I'm dealing with it—by not going anywhere because I don't want to do it. It's hard."

Christina credited her Sweetest Thing costar Selma Blair—who was diagnosed with MS in 2018—for urging her to get tested for the disease.

"She said, 'You need to get checked for MS,'" Christina recalled during her GMA interview. "If not for her, it could've been way worse."

Applegate confirmed that Dead to Me will likely be her last onscreen credit, telling Vanity Fair in May 2023, "I can’t even imagine going to set right now."

"I’m probably not going to work on-camera again, but I'm so glad that I went out with someone who is by far the greatest actress I’ve ever worked with in my entire life," she said of costar Linda Cardellini, "if not the greatest human I’ve ever known.

For Applegate, contracting sapovirus—a virus commonly transmitted through fecal-oral contact—meant having to rely on adult diapers.

"I woke up at three o'clock in the morning in a pool of s--t," she explained on an April 2024 episode of her MeSsy podcast. "I didn't know what happened, and having MS at three o'clock in the morning and trying to change your sheets, it's not fun. But this brings me to my next point, which we can talk about: I'm wearing diapers."

Their discussion was "so liberating," her co-host Sigler noted. "This is something you do by yourself and it feels terrible when you're doing it. I've done that hundreds of times in my MS journey through the years when I've experienced a flair or when I was sick."

"I am in a depression right now, which I don't think I've felt for years," Applegate confessed to Sigler on another 2024 episode of their MeSsy podcast. "Like a real, f--k it all, like real depression where it's kind of scaring me too a little bit because it feels really fatalistic."

Three years into her diagnosis, she continued, "And I'm still sitting here like ‘Boohoo, woe is me.' I'm still mad about it." But the truth was, added, "I don't enjoy living—I don't enjoy things anymore."

Applegate acknowledged the reality of living with MS can be grim. “Everybody has different ways of it showing up,” she noted on a November 2024 episode of her podcast. "I lay in bed screaming. Like, the sharp pains, the ache, the squeezing."

At times, the discomfort even makes it hard for her to enjoy her one source of comfort, the reality TV that at times is "on 24/7 in my room," she noted. But, "I can’t even pick up my phone sometimes," Applegate added, "because now it’s traveled into my hands. So I’ll try to go get my phone or get my remote to turn on the TV or whatever, and sometimes I can’t even hold them."

Since her 2021 diagnosis, Applegate has gotten used to the feel of an open-front gown. "I've been in the hospital upwards of 30 times from throwing up and diarrhea and pain," she relayed on her pocast in March 2025. "That is unimaginable, OK? They’ve done every test known to man on me, put so much radiation into my body from CT scans to everything else."

In the plus column, she noted, she has connected some of her gastro issues to her larger illness. "I have noticed that—and I’m going to be really honest—if I have to poop, I puke," she detailed. "And when I puke, I get all the pain and then all the things happen."

Mere months after her hospital confession, Applegate was checking back in. Returning from a family trip to Europe, "I'm like, 'I wanna be admitted. I'm staying here because I want answers,'" she recounted that August of what she told staff. "'I want every test that you can possibly think of or ones that you haven't even thought of, and I want them done.'"

Following a middle-of-the-night CT, she learned, "I had kidney infections."

More than five years after sharing her battle with multiple sclerosis, she learned that she was misdiagnosed.

"She came into my room and said, 'Congratulations, you don't have MS,'" she recalled of her doctor's visit in the October 2026 podcast MeSsy. "I was, like, not happy at all. Everybody else was so happy."

Applegate emphasized that the confirmation of the misdiagnosis hadn't changed how she's continued to struggle physically.

"I haven't gotten better," she said. "If anything, I've gotten worse. So I'm back at square one where I don't know what's going on with me. That's going to be an online investigation that my doctors are going to be getting into just to find out what has been going on since 2021."

Avatar: The Last Airbender’s Zuko Prequel Is Only 3 Months Away, Here’s Who’s Returning

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

2026 has been an epic year for Avatar: The Last Airbender fans, with recent releases such as Avatar Aang: The Last Airbender and Avatar: Seven Havens. However, the year has also had its fair share of disappointments, most notably the cancellation of Avatar Studios' animated movie starring an adult Fire Lord Zuko. The project was reportedly in development before Paramount ultimately scrapped it in 2026. However, fans are now just three months away from the release of a Zuko-starring prequel, and he isn't returning alone.

We may not be getting the adult Zuko film that OG fans were frothing at the mouth for, but the world of Avatar: The Last Airbender will officially be taking fans back to Zuko's early days in exile with Avatar: The Last Airbender – Enter the Blue Spirit. This upcoming graphic novel from Dark Horse Comics will tell the story of Zuko adopting the Blue Spirit persona for the first time to save a remote Fire Nation colony.

After four years of waiting, fans of Netflix’s Blue Eye Samurai have plenty to look forward to in 2027, with two new releases coming to the franchise.

Brought to life by the creative talents of writer Faith Erin Hicks, artist Peter Wartman, and colorist Adele Matera, this 80-page story will be available for $13.99 on February 23, 2027. It will deliver a missing chapter from Zuko's story, set before Nickelodeon's original TV series. While the story will obviously see the return of the Fire Nation prince, we also have confirmation of two other characters who will appear, along with some speculation about who else we might see.

As a quick recap, Zuko first appears as the Blue Spirit in the original show during Season 1 of Avatar: The Last Airbender, specifically in Book One: Water, Episode 13, "The Blue Spirit." Zuko disguises himself as a masked swordsman to infiltrate Admiral Zhao's fortress and rescue Aang after his capture, giving himself another chance to receive credit for capturing the Avatar and, in turn, restoring his honor.

Now, AtLA is taking fans back in time with Enter the Blue Spirit, revisiting the early days of Zuko's exile and lifting the veil on the origins of his Blue Spirit persona. Below, readers can find the full synopsis for the upcoming graphic novel:

In the early months of Prince Zuko's exile, he and Iroh discover a long-forgotten Fire Nation colony along the coast of the Earth Kingdom. But when the Fire Nation demands larger quantities of the valuable ore the village produces for the war effort, the colonists' lives are suddenly at risk. Desperate to defend his people, even against his own homeland, Zuko becomes the Blue Spirit for the very first time. But if he's discovered thwarting the will of the Fire Lord, he'll lose any chance at regaining his honor and returning home.

Thanks to this synopsis, we have confirmation that fan-favorite Uncle Iroh will be returning in this prequel. This is both unsurprising and reassuring. AtLA fans know that Uncle Iroh was with Zuko from the very beginning of his banishment. The fact that Enter the Blue Spirit respects this piece of canon bodes well for the graphic novel staying true to the series' continuity and respecting the original source material, something longtime fans will undoubtedly expect from such a project.

Given that the premise of this prequel revolves around Zuko's banishment, and the fact that synopsis also makes direct reference to Fire Lord Ozai, we can all but guarantee that the Fire Lord will make an appearance as well, even if it's just through a flashback or in the opening pages of the graphic novel. While these are the only original series characters confirmed to return, we wouldn't be surprised if Azula also appears.

While she obviously wouldn't be accompanying Zuko on his exile, AtLA is no stranger to jumping between characters. We may even see flashbacks featuring Zuko's mother, Ursa, since this was common throughout the series whenever Zuko's story was explored. Other notable characters we might get a glimpse of include Mai and Ty Lee. Of course, this is all just speculation at this point, but we'd love to hear which side character you'd most like to see return in this next Avatar: The Last Airbender project.

Avatar: The Last Airbender – Enter the Blue Spirit TP from Dark Horse Comics will be available on February 23, 2027!

Lauren Sánchez Bezos Matches Her Inky Red Bottoms With a Vintage Two-Tone Chanel Skirt

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Lauren Sánchez Bezos has gone from television news anchor to talk show host to Bezos bride, and now, she’s making her name in the world of picture books. With the release of her debut children’s story, The Fly Who Flew to Space, back in 2024, Sánchez Bezos became a New York Times bestseller. And today, she released a new holiday-themed sequel and third installment in the series, titled The Fly Who Flew to the North Pole, which she promoted this morning with an appearance in New York City.

It may not be the holidays quite yet, but Sánchez Bezos showed off her unique, femme-fatale approach to yuletide style as she stopped by CBS Mornings to celebrate her book’s release. There, many of her dressing hallmarks were on full display. Luxury vintage? Check. A cinched silhouette? Check. Pops of bloody red? Check and check.

For the talk-show visit, Sánchez Bezos looked like a gothic Mrs. Claus in a black, skintight turtleneck from Wolford and a fuzzy black miniskirt that was cinched along the waist with a red tweed band that simulated a cummerbund.

This skirt wasn’t made by North Pole elves, though. Rather, it stemmed from the Chanel Fall/Winter 1994 ready-to-wear collection by Karl Lagerfeld, acting as the latest addition to Sánchez Bezos’s ever-increasing archival stock.

The two-tone mini also acted as the perfect complement to the former journalist’s inky patent-leather heels from Christian Louboutin, which featured the brand’s signature red bottoms.

To complete her look, Mrs. Bezos popped on a pair of oversize aviator sunglasses and silver, bow-shaped earrings. Plus, she carried a clasp clutch printed with the cover of her newly-released book (a cheeky marketing tactic that she’s employed in the past).

No matter the occasion, Sánchez Bezos will find a way to let her personality shine through.
